Nowadays \\g{} for backreferences and \\o{} for octal are\n1793|    recommended to avoid the ambiguities in the old syntax.\n1794|\n1795|    Outside a character class, the digits are read as a decimal number. If the\n1796|    number is less than 10, or if there are that many previous extracting left\n1797|    brackets, it is a back reference. Otherwise, up to three octal digits are\n1798|    read to form an escaped character code. Thus \\123 is likely to be octal 123\n1799|    (cf \\0123, which is octal 012 followed by the literal 3). This is the \"Perl\n1800|    style\" of handling ambiguous octal/backrefences such as \\12.\n1801|\n1802|    There is an alternative disambiguation strategy, selected by\n1803|    PCRE2_EXTRA_PYTHON_OCTAL, which follows Python's behaviour. An octal must\n1804|    have either a leading zero, or exactly three octal digits; otherwise it's\n1805|    a backreference. The disambiguation is stable, and does not depend on how\n1806|    many capture groups are defined (it's simply an invalid backreference if\n1807|    there is no corresponding capture group). Additionally, octal values above\n1808|    \\377 (\\xff) are rejected.\n1809|\n1810|    Inside a character class, \\ followed by a digit is always either a literal\n1811|    8 or 9 or an octal number. */\n1812|\n1813|    case CHAR_1: case CHAR_2: case CHAR_3: case CHAR_4: case CHAR_5:\n1814|    case CHAR_6: case CHAR_7: case CHAR_8: case CHAR_9:\n1815|\n1816|    if (isclass)\n1817|      {\n1818|      /* Fall through to octal handling; never a backreference inside a class. */\n1819|      }\n1820|    else if ((xoptions & PCRE2_EXTRA_PYTHON_OCTAL) != 0)\n1821|      {\n1822|      /* Python-style disambiguation. */\n1823|      if (ptr[-1] <= CHAR_7 && ptr + 1 < ptrend && ptr[0] >= CHAR_0 &&\n1824|          ptr[0] <= CHAR_7 && ptr[1] >= CHAR_0 && ptr[1] <= CHAR_7)\n1825|        {\n1826|        /* We peeked a three-digit octal, so fall through */\n1827|        }\n1828|      else\n1829|        {\n1830|        /* We are at a digit, so the only possible error from read_number() is\n1831|        a number that is too large. */\n1832|        ptr--;   /* Back to the digit */\n1833|\n1834|        if (!read_number(&ptr, ptrend, -1, MAX_GROUP_NUMBER, 0, &s, errorcodeptr))\n1835|          {\n1836|          *errorcodeptr = ERR61;\n1837|          break;\n1838|          }\n1839|\n1840|        escape = -(s+1);\n1841|        break;\n1842|        }\n1843|      }\n1844|    else\n1845|      {\n1846|      /* Perl-style disambiguation. */\n1847|      oldptr = ptr;\n1848|      ptr--;   /* Back to the digit */\n1849|\n1850|      /* As we know we are at a digit, the only possible error from\n1851|      read_number() is a number that is too large to be a group number. If we\n2395|reach an unescaped ']' without the special preceding character, return FALSE.\n2396|\n2397|Originally, this function only recognized a sequence of letters between the\n2398|terminators, but it seems that Perl recognizes any sequence of characters,\n2399|though of course unknown POSIX names are subsequently rejected. Perl gives an\n2400|\"Unknown POSIX class\" error for [:f\\oo:] for example, where previously PCRE\n2401|didn't consider this to be a POSIX class. Likewise for [:1234:].\n2402|\n2403|The problem in trying to be exactly like Perl is in the handling of escapes. We\n2404|have to be sure that [abc[:x\\]pqr] is *not* treated as containing a POSIX\n2405|class, but [abc[:x\\]pqr:]] is (so that an error can be generated). The code\n2406|below handles the special cases \\\\ and \\], but does not try to do any other\n2407|escape processing. the real compile phase, there is some post-processing to be done. */\n8538|\n8539|  if (lengthptr == NULL)\n8540|    {\n8541|    /* If this is the first branch, the firstcu and reqcu values for the\n8542|    branch become the values for the regex. */\n8543|\n8544|    if (*last_branch != OP_ALT)\n8545|      {\n8546|      firstcu = branchfirstcu;\n8547|      firstcuflags = branchfirstcuflags;\n8548|      reqcu = branchreqcu;\n8549|      reqcuflags = branchreqcuflags;\n8550|      }\n8551|\n8552|    /* If this is not the first branch, the first char and reqcu have to\n8553|    match the values from all the previous branches, except that if the\n8554|    previous value for reqcu didn't have REQ_VARY set, it can still match,\n8555|    and we set REQ_VARY for the group from this branch's value. */\n8556|\n8557|    else\n8558|      {\n8559|      /* If we previously had a firstcu, but it doesn't match the new branch,\n8560|      we have to abandon the firstcu for the regex, but if there was\n8561|      previously no reqcu, it takes on the value of the old firstcu. */\n8562|\n8563|      if (firstcuflags != branchfirstcuflags || firstcu != branchreqcuflags >= REQ_NONE)\n8581|        {\n8582|        branchreqcu = branchfirstcu;\n8583|        branchreqcuflags = branchfirstcuflags;\n8584|        }\n8585|\n8586|      /* Now ensure that the field in the BRA item now becoming an\n8602|  offset to the first alternative. If there are no alternatives, it points to\n8603|  the end of the group. The length in the terminating ket is always the length\n8604|  of the whole bracketed item. Return leaving the pointer at the terminating\n8605|  char. */\n8606|\n8607|  if (META_CODE(*pptr) != META_ALT)\n8608|    {\n8609|    if (lengthptr == NULL)\n8610|      {\n8611|      uint32_t branch_length = (uint32_t)(code - last_branch);\n8612|      do\n8613|        {\n8614|        uint32_t prev_length = GET(last_branch, 1);\n8615|        PUT(last_branch, 1, branch_length);\n8616|        branch_length = prev_length;\n8617|        last_branch -= branch_length;\n8618|        }\n8619|      while (branch_length > 0);\n8620|      }\n8621|\n8622|    /* Fill in the ket */\n8623|\n8624|    *code = OP_KET;\n8625|    PUT(code, 1, (uint32_t)(code - start_bracket));\n8626|    code += 1 + LINK_SIZE;\n8627|\n8628|    /* Set values to pass back */\n8629|\n8630|    *codeptr = code;\n8631|    *pptrptr = pptr;\n8632|    *firstcuptr = firstcu;\n8633|    *firstcuflagsptr = firstcuflags;\n8634|    *reqcuptr = reqcu;\n8635|    *reqcuflagsptr = reqcuflags;\n8636|    if (lengthptr != NULL)\n8637|      {\n8638|      if (OFLOW_MAX - *lengthptr < length)\n8639|        {\n8640|        *errorcodeptr = ERR20;\n8641|        return 0;\n8642|        }\n8643|      *lengthptr += length;\n8644|      }\n8645|    return okreturn;\n8646|    }\n8647|\n8648|  /* Another branch follows. (That is,\n8650|  pretend that each branch is the only one.)\n8651|\n8652|  In the real compile phase, insert an ALT node. Its length field points back\n8653|  to the previous branch while the bracket remains open. At the end the chain\n8654|  is reversed. It's done like this so that the start of the bracket has a\n8655|  zero offset until it is closed, making it possible to detect recursion. */\n8656|\n8657|  if (lengthptr != NULL)\n8658|    {\n8659|    code = *codeptr + 1 + LINK_SIZE + skipunits;\n8660|    length += 1 + LINK_SIZE;\n8661|    }\n8662|  else\n8663|    {\n8664|    *code = OP_ALT;\n8665|    PUT(code, 1, (int)(code - last_branch));\n8666|    bc.current_branch = last_branch = code;\n8667|    code += 1 + LINK_SIZE;\n8668|    }\n8669|\n8670|  /* Set the maximum lookbehind length for the next branch (if not in a\n8671|  lookbehind the value will be zero) and then advance past the vertical bar. */\n8672|\n8673|  lookbehindlength = META_DATA(*pptr);\n8674|  pptr++;\n8675|  }\n8676|\n8677|  PCRE2_UNREACHABLE(); /* Control never reaches here */\n8678|}\n8679|\n8680|\n8681|\n8682|/*************************************************\n8683|*          Check for anchored pattern            *\n8684|*************************************************/\n8685|\n8686|/* Try to find out if this is an anchored regular expression. Consider each\n8687|alternative branch. If they all start with OP_SOD or OP_CIRC, or with a bracket\n8688|all of whose alternatives start with OP_SOD or OP_CIRC (recurse ad lib), then\n8689|it's anchored. We haven't enough information\n8702|to catch that case precisely.\n8703|\n8704|At first, the best we could do was to detect when .* was in capturing brackets\n8705|and the highest back reference was greater than or equal to that level.\n8706|However, by keeping a bitmap of the first 31 back references, we can catch some\n8707|of the more common cases more precisely.\n8708|\n8709|... To\n303|get around this, we scan the data string for large quantifiers that follow a\n304|closing parenthesis, and reduce the value of the quantifier to 10, assuming\n305|that this will make minimal difference to the detection of bugs.\n306|\n307|Do the same for quantifiers that follow a closing square bracket, because\n308|classes that contain a number of non-ascii characters can take a lot of time\n309|when matching.\n310|\n311|We have to make a copy of the input because oss-fuzz complains if we overwrite\n312|the original. The *\n310|* first of these tells PCRE2 that an empty string at the start of the    *\n311|* subject is not a valid match; other possibilities must be tried. The   *\n312|* second flag restricts PCRE2 to one match attempt at the initial string *\n313|* position. If this match succeeds, an alternative to the empty string   *\n314|* match has been found, and we can print it and proceed round the loop,  *\n315|* advancing by the length of whatever was found. If this match does not  *\n316|* succeed, we still stay in the loop, advancing by just one character.   *\n317|* In UTF-8 mode, which can be set by (*UTF) in the pattern, this may be  *\n318|* more than one byte.                                                    *\n319|*                                                                        *\n320|* However, there is a complication concerned with newlines.
